Overall, cholera outbreaks had a median cumulative attack rate of 78 cases/100,000 (interquartile range [IQR]: 30-195), and lasted 14 weeks (median, IQR: 8-24), with first-level episodes being slightly longer and with lower burden (table S2). Overall, attack rates and duration differed across ENSO conditions (lower attack rates in El Niño and La Niña than in neutral years), but without a consistent pattern across subregions (table S3).

S1.2 El Niño-Southern Oscillation

S1.2.1 ENSO index and cholera outbreak-association lags

We measure ENSO with the official Oceanic Niño Index (ONI) of the NOAA Climate Prediction Center [42], the three-month running mean of ERSST sea-surface-temperature anomalies in the Niño 3.4 region (5°N-5°S, 120°-170°W) [43, 44]. Writing ONI t for the index of the three-month season centred on calendar month t (the December-February value is assigned to January), the lagged exposures are ONI t-ℓ for candidate lags ℓ = -11, . . . , 11 months, of which lags below -8 months are not selected (Section S1.4.5). A negative lag ℓ reads the index |ℓ| months after the outcome month. As ENSO events develop over several months, the index after the outcome month can describe a developing event better than the index before it, and we therefore let the data choose between positive and negative lags. Each model uses a single lag L, which we select as described in Section S1.4.5.

We summarise the selected lag of each model by its sign. A positive lag L relates the outcome to the ONI L months earlier, so that cholera follows an event that has already developed (a following-year response, as events peak in December-February and cholera responds after the ENSO winter); a zero or negative lag (a lead) relates it to the ONI at or after the outcome, so that cholera responds as the event develops and peaks (a same-year response). The class is assigned per model, so a group can be same-year for one outcome and following-year for another.

S1.3 Statistical modelling framework

The statistical model aims to quantify how ENSO, at a group-specific lag, changes the rate at which outbreaks start, the rate at which they end and the number of cases they produce each month, accounting for seasonality, outbreak history, contagion and persistent differences between places. Every model is fitted separately for each ENSO teleconnection group (Section S1.4.1). We model outbreak occurrence, outbreak duration and monthly case counts with three Bayesian hierarchical models that share the same nested random effects, the same ENSO exposure basis and the same approach to anchoring the intercept on an empirical baseline rate. We first describe this shared structure, then each model, the anchoring, the priors and the estimation settings.

We use one set of symbols in the three models. Index i is an administrative unit (first-or second-level), t a calendar month, k an outbreak episode and s a posterior draw. For a unit i, j(i) and c(i) are the first-level unit and the country containing it; for an episode k, i(k) is the unit in which it was recorded, t 0 (k) its onset month and d kt = t -t 0 (k) + 1 its age in month t. τ (t) ∈ {1, . . . , 12} is the calendar month of the year of month t, ℓ a candidate ENSO lag and L the selected one, h = 1, . . . , J indexes the columns of the ENSO spline basis, u denotes a random intercept and v a random slope. We write a i for the empirical baseline rate of the cell (country and administrative level) containing unit i (Section S1.3.7); each model has its own baseline rate, written a occ i , a dur i and a mon i where the model is not clear from the context. Observations of the occurrence model are unit-months (i, t); observations of the duration and monthly case-count models are episode-months (k, t).

S1.3.1 Discrete-time hazard as a Poisson model

We estimate the occurrence and duration models as Poisson mixed models fitted to a unit-month expansion of the data, the standard approach for piecewise-constant discrete-time hazard models [45, 46].

For an interval (i, t) of length ∆t it days (a unit-month, or an episode-month for duration) with event indicator y it ∈ {0, 1} and a hazard λ it = exp(η it ) constant within the interval, the event probability is and we fit the log-linear Poisson form with log ∆t it as an offset.

With at most one event per interval and ∆t it the time at risk, this likelihood is proportional to the piecewise-exponential survival likelihood with right-censoring [45, 46], and it agrees with the grouped-time likelihood (S1) [47] to first order in λ it ∆t it , as monthly onset and cessation probabilities are small. The simulator draws events from (S1) (Section S1.6.3).

S1.3.2 Hierarchical structure

All three models include nested random intercepts, with random slopes on selected covariates. The nesting reflects the expectation that two administrative units in the same country are more alike than two units in different countries, and the random slopes that the effect of a covariate may also vary geographically.

We fit every model separately for each ENSO group. As the group already stratifies the continent, no model includes a regional level. The occurrence model nests random intercepts at country, first-level unit and second-level unit. The duration and monthly case-count models, fitted like the occurrence model to first-and second-level units, nest them at country and first-level unit, with an additional second-level intercept for observations at second-level units. We estimate random effects as mutually independent variance components.

S1.3.3 ENSO exposure-response

We evaluate the ENSO exposure at a single lag L, selected for each model and ENSO group (Section S1.4.5), and model it as a smooth non-linear function of the index: where b 1 , . . . , b J are the basis functions of a natural cubic spline with J degrees of freedom, β h the population coefficients and v h,c(i) and v h,j(i) the country and first-level-unit random slopes of basis column h.

We write f ENSO for the population curve (v = 0). The natural spline is linear beyond its boundary knots, which limits the curvature of the fitted curve at extreme ONI values. We chose J together with the grouping, from the latent-class models with four to five degrees of freedom (Section S1.4.2); the chosen basis, J = 4, is used in all three models and all groups.

The random slopes on every basis column let the shape of the exposure-response curve vary geographically within a group. The boundary knots are the extremes of the ONI values at lag L over the observations of the group's dataset, so the fit itself involves no extrapolation. The simulations read index values beyond this range, chiefly in strong El Niño events, and clamp the index (Section S1.6.11).

S1.3.4 Occurrence model

We model outbreak occurrence with a Poisson mixed model of the form (S2) fitted to the occurrence dataset, with the baseline rate as a second offset (Section S1.3.7), µ it = ∆t it a occ i exp(η it ), and linear predictor where ϑ it is the day of the unit's cholera year on which month t starts (0 to 365; Section S1.1.6), s(•) is a cyclic cubic regression spline with eight knots, giving a smooth seasonal baseline hazard that joins continuously across the cholera-year boundary, f ENSO,i is the single-lag exposure-response function (S3) at the group-specific lag L, and u c(i) , u j(i) and u i are the nested random intercepts at country, first-level unit and second-level unit.

The coefficients of the outbreak-history and contagion terms vary by unit through random slopes: γ c(i) and β χ,c(i) = β χ + v χ,c(i) add a country slope to the population effects, and a country and a first-level-unit slope.

Time since last outbreak at the unit enters as a categorical variable with seven levels: less than one year, one to two years, and so on up to six years or more. Units without an earlier recorded outbreak are assigned to the last level. The categorical form allows a strongly non-linear effect, large in the first year or two and flat thereafter.

The within-unit contagion indicator χ it equals one when an outbreak episode was ongoing anywhere in the first-level unit j(i) at any time during month t -1.

We define the neighbour pressure V it (ρ) as the distance-weighted share of other first-level units of the same transmission unit with an ongoing outbreak one month earlier: where χ (1) j ′ ,t-1 is the ongoing-outbreak indicator of first-level unit j ′ in month t -1, defined as for χ, d jj ′ is the distance between the centroids of units j and j ′ , T (•) is the transmission unit, and ρ is a bandwidth that we fix at 500 km.

Transmission units are two pre-specified clusters of countries, Central-East and West, within which cholera activity is connected by movement and shared epidemic waves [48] (table S6); they are independent of the ENSO groups, which describe the climate signal rather than the spread of infection. The normalising sum runs over every first-level unit of the transmission unit, including units never affected, so that the covariate is bounded between 0 and 1. As it excludes the focal first-level unit j(i), it measures pressure from neighbours only and remains distinct from χ. Neighbours are coupled across national borders within a transmission unit but not across transmission units, and second-level units take the value of their first-level unit. Time since last outbreak and the within-unit contagion indicator have country random slopes, and the neighbour pressure and each of the J ENSO basis columns have country and first-level-unit random slopes. The seasonal spline does not vary by unit.

S1.3.5 Duration model

We model outbreak duration with a Poisson cessation-hazard model of the same form (S2), fitted to the duration dataset, in which the outcome indicates that an ongoing episode ended in a given month. Episodes enter at the first-or second-level unit in which they were recorded.

Episode k contributes one interval for each calendar month t during which it is active, and we denote this set of months by A k . Each interval carries the indicator y kt ∈ {0, 1} that the episode ended in month t, the number of days e kt > 0 of month t during which the episode was active, floored at one day, and the episode's age d kt , which serves as the baseline clock.

Writing ν kt for the cessation hazard, assumed constant within the month, the likelihood contribution of each interval is where a dur i(k) is the empirical baseline cessation rate (see Section S1.3.7) and log e kt enters as an offset, so that exp(η dur ) is a hazard ratio relative to that baseline.

The cessation log-hazard is where B(•) maps episode age d to one of six bands (months 1, 2 and 3 each form a band, followed by months 4-5, 6-8 and 9 or more), ψ 1 , . . . , ψ 6 are the band effects with ψ 1 ≡ 0 for identifiability, so that the intercept α carries the hazard of the first month, g dur is a seasonal term with two harmonics in the calendar month τ = τ (t) of the active month, capturing an annual and a semi-annual cycle, with four fixed effects θ 1 , . . . , θ 4 without random slopes, f ENSO,i(k) is the single-lag exposure-response function ( S3) with country and first-level-unit random slopes on its J basis columns, and u (•) are nested random intercepts at country and first-level unit, with a second-level intercept for episodes recorded at a second-level unit.

The band effects have their own prior, N (0, 1.5) (table S5). As for the occurrence model, this Poisson likelihood is the survival likelihood with right-censoring [45, 46]: censored episodes contribute months in which the cessation indicator is zero and no terminating month. The forward simulator draws cessation events from the monthly probability 1 -exp(-ν kt e kt ) (Section S1.6.4).

S1.3.6 Monthly case-count model

We model the monthly case count C kt of episode k in month t with a negative binomial mixed model: where e kt is the number of active days as in the duration model, a mon i(k) the empirical baseline rate of the episode's unit, and ζ a population elasticity on the log population log pop i(k) , centred within administrative level.

Population thus enters both as an offset and through ζ, so that case counts need not scale proportionally with population. The seasonal term g mon has the two-harmonic form (S8) of g dur , with its own coefficients. The within-episode epidemic curve f MSO,c is a cubic B-spline with four degrees of freedom in the episode's age d kt (months since onset), with country random slopes on its four columns, and f ENSO,i(k) is the single-lag exposure-response function (S3) with country and first-level-unit random slopes. Random intercepts are nested at country and first-level unit, and observations at second-level units have an additional second-level intercept.

We also model the negative binomial dispersion hierarchically, with country and first-level-unit random intercepts on log ϕ i(k) . This allows overdispersion to vary by geography, as excess variability in reported case counts reflects local surveillance practice as much as local epidemiology.

S1.3.7 Empirical baseline-rate anchoring

In all three models the conditional mean is a product of offsets and an exponentiated linear predictor, µ = ( n O n ) exp(η). We include in each model an additional offset, the empirical baseline rate a, whose denominator equals the product of the model's other offsets:

The intercept then measures the log ratio of the fitted rate to the observed baseline rate, a dimensionless quantity of order one, so that exp(α) ≈ 1 is the appropriate prior centre in every model. The numerator and denominator of a are:

Numerator Denominator (= product of the analysis's other offsets) Occurrence number of onsets days at risk Duration number of episode terminations active days (minimum one) Monthly case counts rounded monthly case count population × active days We estimate baseline rates in cells defined by country and administrative level. For cell m with numerator total Y m and denominator total E m , the raw estimate is log(Y m /E m ). Under Y m ∼ Poisson(E m a m ), the delta-method variance of log(Y m /E m ) is 1/Y m , and we shrink each cell towards its country parent, the rate of its country pooled over administrative levels after adjustment to a common level (below), with the empirical-Bayes weight ), where ξ 2 is the between-cell variance in excess of Poisson sampling noise, which we estimate by moments over cells with at least one event as ξ2 = var(log âm ) -mean(1/Y m ), floored at 10 -6.

Baseline rates also differ systematically by administrative level, as a finer unit has a smaller population and therefore a different event rate per unit time. We estimate this difference between levels within countries (the level contrast), and use it to adjust a cell to a common level before pooling it into a coarser parent and to re-express a borrowed parent rate at the target level. The contrast is the level coefficient of a Poisson regression of the numerator on administrative level and country with the log denominator as offset, in which the country term performs the demeaning. A contrast whose confidence interval includes zero is set to zero.

We resolve the rate of each cell in order of specificity: (i) for a cell with observations, its own shrunken estimate, which for a cell without events is its country parent; (ii) for a cell without observations in a country observed only at other administrative levels, that country's rate adjusted to the target level; (iii) the ENSO-group rate; and (iv) the continent-wide rate. No cell needed (iii) or (iv).

Of the 60 country-by-level cells at the first and second administrative levels, 51 have observations of their own in the occurrence model, 51 in the monthly model and 46 in the duration model, in which 5 further cells have observations but no termination and take the value of their parent. The remaining 9 cells borrow a level-adjusted country rate. In the duration model the between-cell variance is at its floor, indicating no heterogeneity in cessation rates beyond Poisson noise once country and level are accounted for; the shrinkage weights are then close to zero, and every duration cell takes, in effect, the rate of its country parent. All three models retain a second-level contrast.

We compute one baseline-rate table for the whole continent, used by every group and model and by the simulator.

S1.3.8 Priors

Table S5 lists the priors. The intercept α has a normal prior, and every fixed-effect coefficient β, including the ENSO basis coefficients, a normal prior centred at zero. The standard deviations of random intercepts and slopes at the country and administrative-unit levels, the smoothing standard deviation of the seasonal spline and the random-effect standard deviation of the log dispersion have half-normal priors. In the single-country group GG (Nigeria), the country-level random effects are kept, so that the simulator draws them like those of the other groups, under a tight N + (0, 0.05) prior on their standard deviations, as one country cannot inform a between-country variance.

As the intercept measures a log ratio to the empirical baseline rate, its prior puts 95% probability on fitted rates within roughly a factor of seven of the observed baseline rate. The prior on the other fixed effects (all but the duration band effects, whose prior is wider; table S5) puts 95% probability within roughly a factor of 2.7 per unit change in a covariate on the rate scale. We chose the random-effect standard deviation priors based on prior-predictive simulations, which showed that wider scales imply between-unit rate ratios of implausible magnitude. For the seasonal spline of the occurrence model we used N + (0, 0.35) on the smoothing standard deviation, as the heavy-tailed half-t 3 prior with scale 2.5 allows seasonal amplitudes far larger than observed and dominated the residual prior-predictive over-prediction even with the intercept anchored.

S1.3.9 Estimation

We fitted all models with Hamiltonian Monte Carlo as implemented in the Stan programming language [49], with four chains of 350 warm-up and 1,000 sampling iterations, giving 4,000 posterior draws per model. We assessed convergence for every model using the potential scale reduction factor R, bulk and tail effective sample sizes, the number of divergent transitions after warm-up, and the energy-based Bayesian fraction of missing information (GitHub data folder, file D27 convergence, fig. S11).

S1.4 Data-informed ENSO grouping and lag selection

Two structural choices are made from the data: which countries share an ENSO exposure-response (the grouping) and at which lag the index enters each model. Both encode the timing and sign of the ENSO teleconnection, which differ across Africa and are not known a priori precisely enough to fix per country, so we make both by predictive comparison, in two steps. We first estimate the partition of countries with a latent-class extension of the occurrence model, in which every class has its own ENSO lag and exposureresponse curve, choosing the number of classes and the spline basis from a country-block cross-validation comparison and the agreement of the chains (Section S1.4.2). Within this partition, we then select the lag of each model and group by approximate leave-one-out cross-validation over the lag grid, with L ≥ -8 (Section S1.4.5). The class lags of the latent-class model only define the partition. As the same occurrence data choose the partition and fit the group models, we report each country's membership uncertainty beside its effects.

S1.4.1 ENSO teleconnection grouping

The influence of ENSO on African rainfall and temperature differs in sign, strength and timing between regions [20]. We therefore fitted every model separately for each of five ENSO groups of the 30 countries with outbreak data (table S7, fig. S6a).

The groups are data-informed: they are the classes of a latent-class model of outbreak occurrence, in which countries that share an ENSO lag and exposure-response form one class (Section S1.4). They are classes of countries, not necessarily contiguous geographic regions. We name each group after the part of the continent in which most of its members lie, and list them from west to east and south: GW (West), GG (Guinean Gulf), GH (Horn), GE (East) and GS (South). GG holds Nigeria alone; most other countries of the Gulf of Guinea coast are in GW. Table S7 lists the members of each group. Released data files label each group by the a priori regional group whose seed countries fix its class, which keeps the class labels identifiable (Section S1.4.2; table S7).

S1.4.2 Latent-class grouping model

In the latent-class model each country c belongs to one of K classes q = 1, . . . , K, and the ENSO term of a unit-month (i, t) of a country in class q is a Gaussian kernel over the lag grid centred at the class lag µ q , with a fixed width σ ω = 1.5 months and a uniform prior on µ q over [-11, 11], applied to a natural cubic spline of the index with J degrees of freedom.

The rest of the linear predictor is that of the occurrence model (S4) without a regional level, and the class coefficients β (q) have country and first-level-unit random slopes. Class membership is marginalised at country level: the likelihood of a country is the mixture over classes of the likelihood of all its unit-months, with membership probabilities softmax q (log π q + κA cq ), where A is the one-hot encoding of an a priori partition into East Africa, the Horn of Africa, Southern Africa, West Africa and Nigeria (table S7) and κ ≥ 0, with a half-normal prior, measures how strongly the data retain it. Classes are identified by a core of seed countries fixed to their a priori group (Tanzania, Uganda and Burundi; Ethiopia and Sudan; Malawi, Zimbabwe and Zambia; Niger, Liberia and Togo; Nigeria). The seeds identify the classes: without them the mixture is unchanged by a relabelling of its classes, and in exploratory fits the chains swapped class labels or left classes empty. Fixing a core of countries to each class keeps the labels fixed within a chain, so that chains can be compared and averaged class by class. The core of each of the four regional classes is made of the countries that fell in the class of their a priori group in about 85% or more of the chains of exploratory four-class fits seeded with one country per class, which had left some classes with a single member; Uganda and Burundi are fixed with Tanzania, and Nigeria, its own a priori group, seeds the fifth class alone. All other countries are free.

We fitted the model over a grid of natural-spline bases with four to five degrees of freedom and two to five classes, with a priori partitions for fewer than five classes that merge whole groups of the five-group partition, and compared the fits by country-block approximate leave-one-out cross-validation, one block per country (table S8). Each fit ran eight chains of 400 warm-up and 300 sampling iterations. The latent-class model is multimodal, so we also required the chains of a fit to agree: we retained the three chains whose class lags agree best (the smallest maximum difference between their class lags), and summarised every fit on these chains. A mode is a set of chains whose class lags agree within 1.5 months; the mode sizes of every fit are given in table S8.

We selected the final fit, with four degrees of freedom and K = 5 classes, because its three agreeing chains form its dominant mode and its difference in expected log predictive density to the best fit of the comparison, -16.4 (standard error 14.2), is about one standard error. The best fit, with five degrees of freedom and five classes, has no mode of three chains. Each country is assigned to its class of highest mean posterior membership probability over the agreeing chains, and we write p max for that probability.

Fig. S6c shows how the classes change as K rises from 2 to 4 at the selected basis, and panel b the final fit with K = 5. We describe each class by the final groups of its members: a class written GW + GG holds the countries of both groups, and "part of" gives the share of a group's countries that the class holds. The most probable classes are, at K = 2: GW + GG + GH, GE + GS; at K = 3: part of GW (10 of 15) + GH + GS, part of GW (5 of 15) + GG, GE; at K = 4: part of GW (10 of 15) + GS, part of GW (5 of 15) + GG, GH, GE; at K = 5: GW, GG, GH, GE, GS. The final partition keeps part of the regional structure of the a priori partition, and two of the 30 countries are assigned to a class other than the one their a priori group seeds (table S7). The countries of the WHO African region without outbreak data are not assigned to any group.

One of the 30 countries, Ghana, has p max < 0.6, and the class sizes summed over the membership probabilities differ from the hard counts (fig. S6b). The seed countries have p max = 1 by construction. We report p max beside every group and country effect (Figs. 1 and 2; table S9; fig. S6b; data S1, files T3a effects country occurrence, T3b effects country duration and T3c effects country monthly), the stability of the partition under sampling from the membership probabilities, and its sensitivity to the number of classes, the exposure basis and the chains used: adjusted Rand index and countries that change class after an optimal matching of class labels, with the country-block difference in expected log predictive density to the final fit (GitHub data folder, files D5 membership stability and D5 membership sensitivity; fig. S6d).

S1.4.3 Rainy seasons of the teleconnection groups

Panel c of Fig. 1 in the main text outlines the main rainy season or seasons of each teleconnection group. For each first-level administrative unit we computed the monthly rainfall climatology, the mean CHIRPS rainfall [50] of each calendar month over 1990-2019, from the admin-level climate dataset of the VIMC climate project [51] (for units without their own series, the mean over their second-level units). A calendar month is rainy in a unit when its climatological rainfall exceeds the unit's average month, one twelfth of its annual total [52]; units receiving less than 100 mm a year have no rainy season. A month belongs to a group's main season when at least half of the group's 2025 population, from the population estimates described in Section S1.1.5, lives in units in which that month is rainy. Weighting by population rather than counting units prevents sparsely populated arid units from shaping a group's season. Each run of consecutive such months, allowed to cross the year end, is outlined as one season, so a group with two rainy seasons shows two outlines. Months close to the 50% threshold could fall on either side of it under a different population year or climatology period.

S1.4.4 Seasonality of outbreaks, cessation and cases

We compared the rainy seasons of Section S1.4.3 with the monthly pattern of each outcome, by ENSO group and calendar month (the fitted seasonal patterns are shown in panel c of Fig. 1 in the main text). For occurrence we computed the number of outbreak onsets per 1,000 unit-months at risk in the occurrence dataset, by the calendar month of the onset. For cessation we computed the number of episodes ending per 100 active episode-months in the duration dataset. For case counts we took the median and interquartile range of suspected cases per active episode-month in the monthly dataset, leaving out months with a reporting gap.

All three are computed on the units the models are fitted to (Section S1.1.8). Onset and cessation rates carry exact Poisson 95% intervals. A month with fewer than five events is not used to locate a peak. Observed onsets peak in a rainy-season month in all five groups, cessation in one of the five groups and cases per active month in three of the five groups.

S1.4.5 ENSO lag selection

Once the country partition was established, for each model and ENSO group, we fitted the single-lag model (S3) separately at every candidate lag L ∈ {-11, . . . , 11} months, and compared the fits by approximate leave-one-out cross-validation with Pareto-smoothed importance sampling [53]. For each lag we computed the expected log pointwise predictive density relative to the best lag, together with the standard error of the paired difference.

A negative lag is admissible because the index after the outcome month is a function of the developing ENSO event, which can precede its own peak by several months.

We select for each model and group the lag with the highest expected log predictive density among the lags allowed by a floor, L ≥ -8. The floor keeps the index a model reads within the forecast's reach: the firm forecast horizon of a group is the last month of the ENSO forecast plus its smallest lag (Section S1.6.12), so a lag of -9 to -11 months would end it within a few months of the launch. The duration and monthly case-count lags are selected independently of the occurrence lag. Selected lags are given in table S9, and full rankings, with the Pareto k diagnostics of every lag, in the GitHub data folder, file D7 lag loo ranking and fig. S7. We call two lags distinguishable only when their difference exceeds two standard errors of the paired difference; the rankings give, for every model and group, the number of lags within two standard errors of the best, and in several of them the selected lag is a point choice among nearly equivalent lags.

The floor binds in three of the 15 combinations of model and group (duration model of GH, best lag -9; monthly case-count model of GH, best lag -10; monthly case-count model of GG, best lag -11). Table S9 reports for every model and group the floor, whether the best lag lies below it and the paired difference in expected log predictive density that this costs, the second-best lag and the best non-negative lag, each with its difference to the best lag. Fig. S7 shades the lags below the floor.

S1.5 Derived quantities

S1.5.1 Exposure-response curves

We reconstruct posterior exposure-response curves draw by draw. For each posterior draw s, we combine the fitted basis coefficients β (s) 1 , . . . , β (s) J with the spline basis evaluated on a grid of ONI values, and express the result as an incidence rate ratio relative to the reference ONI0 = 0, the centre of ENSO-neutral conditions:

We summarise curves by the pointwise median and the equal-tailed 50% and 95% credible intervals of IRR (s) (ONI) across draws. Group-level curves use the fixed-effect coefficients, and country-and first-levelunit curves add the corresponding random slopes to β

S1.5.2 Population living in units with an ENSO effect

For an ENSO state ONI 1 ∈ {-1.5, -1, 1, 1.5, 2}, a first-level unit has an effect of magnitude m in a model when its unit-level rate ratio (S13) at ONI 1 lies above 1 + m or below 1/(1 + m). The effect is adverse when it means more cholera: onset and case rate ratios above 1 + m, and a cessation hazard ratio below 1/(1 + m), hence longer outbreaks. It is protective in the opposite direction, and we also count effects in either direction. We evaluate magnitudes m ∈ {0, 0.10, 0.25, 0.50} and posterior probability cut-offs p ∈ {0.5, 0.8, 0.95}, for each of the three models alone and for any of them.

A unit is counted at (m, p) when the model, taken on its own, gives a posterior probability of at least p to an effect of magnitude m in the chosen direction; for "any model", when at least one of the three models does. The headline count is the population of the units with an adverse effect in any of the three models at m = 0.25 and p = 0.95. It is complemented by the expected population with an effect, the posterior mean and 95% interval over draws of the population of the units whose drawn rate ratio lies beyond the same bound, and by the counts at every other magnitude, cut-off and direction (data S1, files D10 enso exposure exceedance and D12 enso exposure by country). Populations are the 2025 estimates of each unit of the dataset (Section S1.1.5).

S1.6 Simulation

We use the fitted models to simulate outbreak histories forward in time under factual and counterfactual ENSO trajectories. This section describes the simulator, the particle filter that reconstructs the historical state, and the hindcast, forecast and attribution designs.

S1.6.1 Notation

Symbol

Meaning i simulation unit (second-level administrative unit, or first-level unit without a reporting second-level unit), i = 1, . . . , I j(i), c(i), G(i), T (i) the first-level unit, country, ENSO group and transmission unit containing i t month index, t = 1, . . . , M ; n t is its length in days k episode index s posterior draw, s = 1, . . . , S r replicate, r = 1, . . . , R x it exogenous covariate vector (ENSO at the selected lag, seasonal terms) at (i, t) Y it simulated onset indicator at (i, t) A it simulated active indicator: an episode is ongoing at (i, t) A d it simulated detected-activity indicator: a detected episode or a false alarm at (i, t) D it months elapsed since the onset of the currently active episode U it time elapsed since the last outbreak at i, in days (binned to the fitted model's categories at the point of use) C it simulated case count at (i, t) θ (s) the s-th posterior draw, comprising all fixed effects, random effects and dispersion parameters of the three fitted models pop it , a i population at i and the empirical baseline rate of its cell, one per model Symbol Meaning χ it , V it (ρ) within-unit contagion indicator and neighbour pressure at (i, t)

S1.6.2 State vector and its dynamics

The simulator is a discrete-time Markov chain with state at the end of month t

All other quantities needed by the models at month t + 1 are deterministic functions of S t and of the exogenous covariates. As the models were fitted to recorded episodes, the contagion covariates and the time since last outbreak are built from detected rather than latent activity. Each simulated episode is detected at onset with probability 1 -δ fn and keeps this status while it lasts, and an inactive unit records a false alarm with monthly probability δ fp , with δ fn and δ fp as in the measurement model of Section S1.6.8; A d it = 1 for a detected episode or a false alarm. We compute the two contagion covariates from the detected indicators as where is the detected state of first-level unit j and w is the distance kernel of (S5), restricted to the transmission unit.

As in the fitted model, χ includes the unit's own activity and V excludes it.

In the fitted data, the neighbour covariate counts recorded activity only, so a neighbour without surveillance in the previous month counts as inactive. We measured this coverage without changing the covariate. A unit was under surveillance in a month if one of its location periods had a reporting window covering it (including first-level series), the month was a national-zero month, or the unit had a recorded episode then. For each unit-month we computed the kernel-weighted share of its neighbours under surveillance in the previous month, over the same kernel and transmission-unit restriction as the covariate. In the simulations every unit is simulated, so the implicit coverage there is one, and the simulator applies the contagion effect at a neighbour activity that the fit may not have observed where coverage was low. Coverage by country, group and year is given in GitHub data folder, file D33 contagion coverage. Time since last outbreak advances at each month without a detected onset, and resets at a detected onset; false alarms feed the contagion covariates but do not reset it: where we advance the counter by the mean month length δ rather than by the calendar length n t , as it only feeds a coarse categorical coding.

A unit with no recorded onset is coded in the oldest category (six years or more), as in the fitted model. Months since onset advance while an episode is active and are undefined otherwise.

The within-year clock of the seasonal baseline hazard resets at each location's trough month, but the state variables do not: recent outbreak history and ongoing outbreaks carry over across the cholera-year boundary in calendar time.

As the distance kernel is zero between transmission units (Section S1.3.4), no state variable couples them: transmission units evolve independently given a posterior draw and the covariates, and are simulated in parallel. Each holds 15 modelled countries of several ENSO groups (table S6). Each country is simulated with the models, selected lags and posterior draws of its own ENSO group, while the contagion terms couple it to its neighbours.

S1.6.3 Occurrence step

At each month, we evaluate the occurrence model for every unit not currently experiencing an episode, given the current state and the covariates: where it is given by (S4) evaluated at draw s.

Following (S1), the probability of an onset during the month is

The simulator follows the fitted risk set, from which a unit leaves after an onset until the next cholera year (Section S1.1.7): the hazard (S16) is that of the first onset of a cholera year, so we draw an onset only for a unit that is inactive and has had no detected onset yet in its current cholera year. With τ cy it ∈ {0, . . . , 11} the month of the unit's cholera year, counted from its first month, this holds when U it / δ ≥ τ cy it , U it being read before its update (S15), or when the unit has no detected onset at all. A unit whose episode ends within the cholera year is therefore not at risk again before the next one, and simulated detected onsets, like the fitted data and the observations the hindcasts are scored against, number at most one per unit and cholera year. An undetected episode does not close the risk set, as an unrecorded episode does not in the fitted data.

S1.6.4 Duration step

For an episode k active at (i, t) with D it months since onset, we evaluate the cessation hazard from the duration model at draw s, where η dur,(s) it is given by (S7) with its band term evaluated at the simulated episode age D it and its seasonal term at the calendar month of t.

The episode ends during the month with probability q (s) where e it is the number of days of month t during which the episode is active.

As the simulator does not resolve days within a month, it sets e it to the length of the month, halved in the onset month (the expected active fraction under a uniformly distributed day of onset) and, in the case-count step (S21), also halved in the month the episode ends, whose cessation is drawn before the case count, as in the fitted dataset, where an episode's first and last months are partial. The cessation probability itself carries only the onset correction, as whether the month is the last is what it draws. Episode durations therefore follow a discrete-time survival distribution, in which the probability that an episode starting in month t 0 is still active after d months is and the expected duration in months is E[D] = d≥0 P (D > d).

As the cessation hazard combines a categorical function of months since onset with the calendar-month harmonics (see Section S1.3.5), (S20) reproduces the non-geometric empirical distribution of durations, in which few episodes end in their first month and the monthly probability of ending levels off from the third, and the expected duration depends on the month of onset.

S1.6.5 Case-count step

For each month in which an episode is active, we draw a case count from the monthly model, where η(s) it collects the non-offset terms of (S10) (intercept, population elasticity, seasonality, within-episode curve, ENSO and random effects) evaluated at draw s, with the within-episode curve f MSO evaluated at the simulated D it .

The negative binomial is parametrised such that Var(C) = µ + µ 2 /ϕ. The mean and the drawn count are both capped at 15% of the unit's population per month.

In the simulator and the historical reconstruction, the population pop it of a unit is resolved for every simulated month at that month's cholera year, the calendar year of the month m i -1 months before t, with m i the unit's trough month (see Section S1.1.6). This is the convention under which the monthly case-count model was fitted. Populations come from the unit population table (2015-2030), and years outside it are rescaled by the national totals of the UN World Population Prospects 2024. The population offset, the population elasticity term and the incidence cap therefore follow population growth over the simulated period. Per-capita rates (annualised incidence and attributable cases per 100,000) divide by the month-weighted mean population over the months each estimate covers, and counts of people living in affected areas use the 2025 population.

S1.6.6 Algorithm

We simulate each replicate r independently for each transmission unit, such that replicate-unit pairs can be distributed across processors. Each replicate uses a single posterior draw s r for all three models of every ENSO group in the transmission unit, so that occurrence, duration and case-count parameters come from a coherent posterior state. The initial state S 0 is a terminal state of the particle filter (see Section S1.6.8). At each month t = 1, . . . , M , we compute the covariates x it and the contagion covariates from S t-1 using (S14). We then draw onsets with (S17) for units in the risk set (Section S1.6.3), cessation for active units with (S19), and case counts for units active during the month with (S21), and update A, A d , D and U to obtain S t . Each replicate returns its event table, with the onset month, duration and total cases of every episode, and its unit-by-month state array.

Detection only feeds the covariates. Every reported simulated quantity, in the reconstruction, the hindcasts, the forecasts and the attribution, counts latent onsets and cases, detected or not.

Random number streams are seeded by replicate, so results do not depend on parallelisation. With Y (r) it and C (r) it the onsets and cases of replicate r and R the number of replicates (Section S1.6.7), the marginal onset probability at (i, t) it , and the expected total number of cases R -1 r t C (r) it , with credible intervals given by empirical quantiles across replicates.

S1.6.7 Uncertainty decomposition

For a scalar summary Z of a simulated trajectory, for instance the total number of outbreaks in a country over a horizon, the total variance decomposes as

Replicates at a fixed posterior draw resolve the first term, and replicates across posterior draws the second. The forward simulations nest replicates within draws in a fixed cycle: replicate r runs posterior draw s r = ((r -1) mod S) + 1 of a fixed set of S draws, so that every draw is run by R/S replicates. The forecast, the counterfactual, the attribution factual and the past-event arms run R = 10,000 replicates per transmission unit and arm, S = 100 draws of R/S = 100 replicates each (GitHub data folder, file D34 run design), and the factual and counterfactual arms of a replicate share its draw. The hindcasts run 5,000 replicates per run in the same cycle, 50 replicates per draw. Intervals over replicates reflect the sum (S22); the intervals of the attributable fraction separate the second term (Section S1.6.14).

S1.6.8 Particle filtering for historical state reconstruction

Forecasts and counterfactuals require a starting state consistent with the observed record. We therefore reconstruct the latent state of the simulator over the observed record, from January 2011 to August 2026, by sequential Monte Carlo before any forward simulation, with the sub-national database as observation to December 2024 and the WHO monthly national series from January 2023 to August 2026. The reconstruction ends in the last month with a published ONI season, so that the forecasts start from the latest observed ENSO state. The ENSO covariates of the reconstruction are the observed ONI throughout; for models with a negative lag, the months after August 2026 are taken from the calibrated ENSO forecast described below.

We write X t = (X 1t , . . . , X It ) for the latent state of all units at month t, where X it comprises the active and detected-activity indicators, the months since onset, the time since last outbreak and the current case-count mean. The transition density f (X t | X t-1 ; θ (s) ) is the one-step simulator described above, evaluated at a fixed posterior draw. The observations at (i, t) are the reported onset indicator Ỹit and, where available, the reported case count Cit.

As reporting is imperfect, we allow for false negatives, onsets that are not recorded, with probability δ fn , and false positives, recorded onsets without a latent onset, with probability δ fp . The onset term of the measurement model is where Y it is the latent onset indicator.

The term is evaluated in the unit-months of the occurrence dataset; that dataset, with the months removed inside running episodes and its national-zero months (Section S1.1.8), sets the risk set of the filter.

The case term is evaluated in every unit-month with a recorded count, including months after an onset, with zeros recorded in at-risk months without a case report. Writing µ it and ϕ i for the mean and dispersion of the monthly model, it is where A it is the latent active indicator.

In an inactive unit a count is reported with probability ε, drawn from a negative binomial with a floor mean µ0 = 0.1, so that the term is a probability distribution over the reported count in both states. The first case penalises latent episodes that continue through months without recorded cases, and the fourth particles whose episode has ended while cases are still reported. We set ε = 1, so that the inactive term is the floor negative binomial at every count; ε cannot be estimated, as every at-risk month outside the recorded episodes is recorded as zero. Missing observations do not contribute to the likelihood. We set δ fn = 0.10 and δ fp = 0.001, reflecting the assumption that under-ascertainment is possible and that spurious outbreak reports are rare.

The WHO monthly national series enters as a third, country-level term. Writing W ct for the national count of country c in month t and C sim ct = i:c(i)=c C it for the particle's simulated cases summed over the modelled units of the country, the term is where w c is a country multiplier and ϕ W = 5 is a reporting overdispersion distinct from the dispersion of the monthly model, as the process variation is already in C sim .

With φ c the share of the national population living in the modelled units of the country, the multiplier is by default w c = 1/φ c , which scales the simulated count to the national total. Where WHO and the database both observe a country over enough months in 2023-2024 (at least 100 database cases over at least 6 overlap months), we estimate its own scale instead: with W c the WHO total and DB c the database total over the simulated units in those months, ς c = W c φ c /DB c , clamped to [0.1, 10], and w c = ς c /φ c , which equals W c /DB c unless clamped. This identified 2 of 30 countries; the others keep the fixed factor 1/φ c (GitHub data folder, file D30 who scale). To avoid scoring a country-month twice, the WHO count is not scored in country-months the database observes. The floor mean of one case gives a particle without simulated activity in the country a finite, graded penalty when cases are reported. This term is evaluated from January 2023; from January 2025, after the end of the sub-national database, it is the only observation, and countries without WHO data run unconstrained from then on. Scoring the simulated count rather than its expectation lets the filter also condition on the sampling variation of the case-count model.

We use the block particle filter [54, 55] implemented in the spatPomp package [30], with one block per country, so that the national term enters its block exactly once, one independent filter run per transmission unit, 30,000 particles, 100 replicates and a 24-month burn-in (January 2009 to December 2010) before the filtering window.

Smoothing replicate j runs the filter at draw j of the 100 posterior draws in every transmission unit, and returns one state path over the whole record. At the last month, the filter samples one particle per block and traces its ancestry back in time, so that the path is a draw from the smoothing distribution, stitched across the countries of the transmission unit, and its state in any month is conditioned on the whole record, later months included. We call these paths the historical reconstruction. Their last states are the initial states S 0 of the forecasts; the hindcasts and the past-event arms start from an earlier month of the same paths. Forward replicate r starts from the state of smoothing replicate s r (Section S1.6.7), one smoothing replicate per posterior draw, and runs the draw s r under which that state was reconstructed, so that state and parameters are a joint posterior draw. The pairing depends only on the replicate number, and is the same in every transmission unit and arm.

S1.6.9 Validation statistics

We compare simulated and observed outbreak histories over their common period, restricted to unit-months with documented surveillance coverage. The same scores serve the hindcasts (Section S1.6.10).

We assess the calibration of the binary monthly onset outcome with the randomised probability integral transform for discrete outcomes [56], which is uniform on (0, 1) when the predictive distribution is calibrated. A U-shaped histogram indicates under-dispersed predictions, a hump-shaped one over-dispersed predictions, and a sloped one a systematic bias.

We measure predictive accuracy for onset counts and case totals with the continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) [57], estimated from the simulated ensemble with the fair estimator, which does not favour ensembles of a particular size [58]. Lower scores are better, and the score rewards sharpness subject to calibration.

We also perform four structural checks comparing simulated and observed month-of-year onset frequencies across the modelled units, the distribution of episode durations, the fraction of unit-months in the active state, and the probability of an onset given an ongoing outbreak in the previous month in the same first-level unit, in another first-level unit of the same country, or in another country of the same transmission unit (fig. S15). The reconstruction of each country is shown in figs. S16 and S17.

S1.6.10 Hindcast skill

We assess retrospective forecast skill with hindcasts. The hindcast years lie inside the fitting period, and the grouping, the lags and the parameters were estimated with them, whereas the reference forecasts leave the scored season out (below). Each hindcast also starts from the historical reconstruction, which has seen the observations of the season it scores (Section S1.6.8), and it reads the observed ENSO index over the scored season rather than a forecast available at launch. The scores are therefore in-sample upper bounds on the skill of the model given perfect knowledge of ENSO; they check consistency and do not establish the skill of a forecast issued in real time (Section S2.1).

Hindcasts follow the cholera season of each country, from trough to trough. A country's trough is the calendar month with the lowest onset rate (onsets per at-risk unit-month, three-month circular smoothing) in the occurrence dataset over 2010-2024, where the country has at least 30 onsets and the trough is stable (within one month in at least 80% of 500 year-bootstrap resamples), and otherwise the trough month shared by most of its units in the cholera-season calendar (Section S1.1.6). This trough, estimated from the onsets themselves, can differ from the calendar trough that sets the cholera years of the models and the past-event windows. The season window runs from the month after the trough to the next trough month inclusive, one month later than a cholera year that starts at the same trough. Its start is floored at May, after the spring predictability barrier of ENSO, and the months cut by the floor and their share of onsets are reported (GitHub data folder, file D3 season ends). Each country is scored to the end of its season window, extended by the smallest of the three selected lags of its group when that lag is positive; the past-event windows are extended by the largest of the three instead (Section S1.6.16).

For each cholera year from 2012 to 2022, we run one hindcast per transmission unit and launch month. These are the first and last cholera years that fit entirely inside the January 2010 to December 2024 data window with their margins: the launch state needs the reconstructed months and the lagged ONI before it, and the season window, extended by the lag, must close before the data end. In each run, the countries whose season starts in that month are launched together from the state of the preceding month in the historical reconstruction, the whole transmission unit is simulated, and only those countries are scored in that run. The observed ONI is used both over the window and in the months before launch that enter its initial state.

We score occurrence on the number of onsets per unit over each season window, in units and months at risk. Its reference is a climatology of the unit itself: a binomial distribution over the unit's at-risk months in the window, with the unit's own onset rate over its at-risk months outside the scored season, so that the scored season does not enter its own reference; its CRPS is computed exactly. We score case counts per month and per season total on the at-risk units, against a pooled monthly climatology: for each scored month, the cases of the same calendar month and its two neighbours in the other cholera years, leaving out the scored season and every season overlapping it, scaled to the population of the scored year. The model ensemble is scored with the fair CRPS (Section S1.6.9) on raw counts and on log(1 + y), and with a threshold-weighted CRPS that counts errors above the 50-case outbreak threshold. Skill is CRPSS = 1 -CRPS/ CRPS ref , with both sums over the same units, months and seasons, pooled by ENSO group, for Africa and by country. Leads count from each country's own launch and are grouped into early (0-3 months), peak-season (4-8; labelled "peak" in the figures, not to be confused with the peak of an ENSO event) and late (9 or more) classes. Intervals come from 1,000 bootstrap resamples of whole cholera years (figs. S18 and S19). Fig. S19a shows the skill against the two climatologies by ENSO group and lead class, for onsets and for monthly case counts on both scales. The hindcasts tend to over-predict case totals: the observed total of a group window (one ENSO group and launch year) falls below the 95% prediction interval in 11 of the 55 group windows and above it in two (fig. S19b).

S1.6.11 Forecast design

Forecasts start in September 2026 from the filtered state at the end of the observed record (August 2026), with the replicates and draws of the forward arms (Section S1.6.7). This is the forecast shown in the figures; the attribution statistics use a second factual arm launched with the counterfactual (Section S1.6.13).

Over the forecast horizon, we draw the ENSO input from the 40 member trajectories of the latest NCEP Climate Forecast System version 2 (CFSv2) monthly Niño 3.4 forecast issued by the NOAA Climate Prediction

S1.6.12 Analogue continuation of the ENSO forecast

Beyond the last month whose ONI is a full three-month CFSv2 mean (the month before the last CFSv2 target month), each replicate's trajectory is continued with an analogue drawn from unforced climate-model simulations. We built a library from the Niño 3.4 series of the pre-industrial control runs published with Lenssen et al. [65] (CMIP6 and earlier NMME and CMIP5-generation models), each rescaled to the standard deviation of the observed ONI over 1991-2020, and characterised every month of every run by its calendar month, its ONI, its three-month tendency and its ONI six months earlier. For a replicate whose CFSv2 trajectory ends in state (v, ∆v, v -6 ), we took the library months of the same calendar month, ranked them by the standardised Euclidean distance over these three features, keeping at most one start per two years within a run, and drew one of the 30 nearest. The continuation follows the analogue's subsequent months, with the offset between the replicate's last value and the analogue's start fading with a three-month time constant. In a validation on 468 observed seams between 1982 and 2020, this continuation scored better (fair CRPS 0.22, 0.40 and 0.43 at leads of 1-6, 7-12 and 13-24 months) than persistence with autoregressive decay (0.44, 0.75, 0.80), climatology (0.47, 0.47, 0.46) and a neutral continuation (0.67, 0.66, 0.65). One run (GFDL CM2.1-NMME) was excluded from the library because it is the longest run, supplying a third of the starts, and has the strongest cold bias after strong El Niño events; its exclusion improved the score after such events from 0.42 to 0.38 at leads of 7-12 months. The library therefore holds 10 runs, and the same runs supply the counterfactual analogues (Section S1.6.13).

A forward simulation covers every month from its launch to the end of its horizon, both included, and starts from the reconstructed state of the month before the launch. As each model uses ENSO at its selected lag, a group's firm horizon is the last month whose ONI is a full three-month CFSv2 mean plus the smallest lag selected for that group across the three models; with a negative lag it falls before that month, and a group whose firm horizon equals the launch month keeps one firm month (table S9, fig. S21, GitHub data folder, file D6 firm horizons). Months past a group's firm horizon read the analogue continuation of ENSO. A transmission unit mixes ENSO groups and is simulated to the latest firm horizon among its countries, with the analogue continuation supplying the ENSO input where needed. Summaries by group, and the country panels of figs. S22 and S23, censor each country at the firm horizon of its own group; the attribution of the 2026 forecast reads every group over one calendar window for Africa (see Section S1.6.13). Each replicate thus combines one ENSO trajectory, one posterior draw and one filtered terminal state.

S1.6.13 Counterfactual design

In the counterfactual arm we replace the ENSO trajectory over the forecast horizon with trajectories of unforced internal ENSO variability, drawn from the pre-industrial control archive of Lenssen et al. [65]. With the neutral analogue set of the main analysis, the difference between factual and counterfactual forecasts gives the cholera outbreak burden attributable to the observed and forecast ENSO state relative to ENSO-neutral conditions; with the full pool of analogues, it is the burden relative to unforced internal ENSO variability of either sign (below).

The counterfactual trajectories are anchored in May 2026, the last month before the 2026-27 El Niño developed, and the counterfactual arm is launched from the filtered state at the end of April 2026, so that the counterfactual ENSO departs from the observed ONI in the same month in which the epidemic state is released from the observations. Every attribution statistic compares this arm with an attribution factual: the factual arm launched from the same end-of-April state, with the same smoothing replicate, random number seed and posterior draw for each replicate, so that the two arms differ only in their ENSO trajectory. The forecast shown in the figures is a third arm, launched from the August 2026 state, which retains the national counts observed from May to August 2026; it enters no attributable quantity. The difference between the displayed forecast and the counterfactual decomposes per posterior draw into an ENSO component (attribution factual minus counterfactual) and an information component (displayed forecast minus attribution factual), the latter being the effect of the months observed between the two launches (GitHub data folder, files D22 attrib information scope and D22 attrib information decomposition). The end-of-April state is itself a smoothed state, reconstructed from observations to August 2026, so the information component compares the realised May to August record with its re-simulation, not with a forecast made in April.

The counterfactual analogues come from the same library as the factual continuation (Section S1.6.12), with the same rescaling, features and excluded run. From the last observed month before the launch, we select the 100 analogues per model nearest in ONI level, three-month tendency and ONI six months earlier, standardised by the library's feature standard deviations and at least 12 months apart within a model, over 10 models. Each path is joined to the observed ONI of the launch month, with the offset between analogue and observation decaying with an e-folding time of three months, so that both arms start from the observed ONI and differ only through the analogues' evolution.

We classify every December to February (DJF) winter read by a reported month (the month minus the group's ENSO lag) on the mean of the monthly ONI over December to February, with thresholds of -0.5 and +0.5 °C and no persistence requirement. A trajectory belongs to the La Niña, neutral or El Niño set if its first winter has that phase and every later winter it reads is neutral; otherwise it is labelled mixed and kept only in the full pool. For the 2026 forecast, the pool of 1,000 trajectories gives 30 La Niña, 151 neutral and 208 El Niño analogues; set sizes, model shares, seam errors and the mean ONI of every classified winter are given in GitHub data folder, files D29 analog sets, D29 analog sets winters and D29 analog sets models. The main analysis draws counterfactuals from the neutral set, which asks what the coming season would hold without an ENSO event of either sign; the full pool and the two other conditional sets are reported in the supplementary figures.

We write N F r and N C r for a burden summary, either the number of outbreaks or total cases, under the factual and counterfactual arms in replicate r, using the same posterior draw and random number stream in both arms, and report two estimands. The first is the ratio of expectations, which we map on a log 10 scale.

We use the ratio of expectations rather than the expectation of ratios because most administrative units have no outbreak in most replicates, so that per-replicate ratios are mostly undefined or infinite. The second is the paired difference, which is defined in every replicate and provides the uncertainty statement: we report a shift as credible when a central interval of the empirical distribution of {∆ r } excludes zero.

Pairing removes the stochastic and parameter variation shared by the two arms, such that Var[∆] is much smaller than Var[N F ] + Var[N C ]. Probability-of-increase maps are not paired. Results for each analogue set are shown in figs. S24 and S25, and the forecast and neutral-ENSO counterfactual of each country in figs. S22 and S23.

The attribution covers one calendar window for every ENSO group, from the attribution start to October 2027 (the firm horizon of group GS, ENSO lag +4 in the occurrence model), and summaries for Africa as a whole sum the groups over that window. Groups whose firm horizon ends earlier are read on into the analogue continuation of ENSO, both arms alike, and groups forecast further are cut at the window end; GitHub data folder, file D23 africa scope gives each group's longest continuation lead per model and the skill of the analogue continuation against climatology at that lead. The counterfactual constrains every winter read by the reported months to be neutral.

S1.6.14 Attribution metrics

We summarise the paired factual and counterfactual simulations with five metrics, each answering a different question. All are computed from the same replicates, per first-level administrative unit and for each country, ENSO group and Africa as a whole. For a first-level unit j and replicate r, N F j,r and N C j,r are the total cases or the number of outbreak onsets over the comparison window in the factual and counterfactual arms. Scope totals sum their units within each replicate, N F z,r = j∈z N F j,r ; countries and ENSO groups use the replicates paired within their group, and Africa uses the replicates present in every group, so that no total mixes draws from different replicates. The main results keep every replicate. As a sensitivity analysis, a replicate pair is removed from every unit of an ENSO group (and from Africa) when, in either arm, any unit of the group reaches 2 × 10 5 cases in a month of the window or the cumulative cases of the group over the whole window reach 10 6 ; in the posterior intervals below, the pair is removed from every group of its transmission unit, so that the groups of a unit keep one set of replicates. As this conditions on the outcome, and the factual arm reaches these thresholds more often where ENSO raises incidence, it pulls the attributable fraction towards zero. The pairs removed because of each arm and the resulting change in the attributable fraction are given in GitHub data folder, file D24 explosive pairs. We report two comparison windows: the main window, from the common launch in May 2026, and, as a sensitivity analysis, a window restricted to the months after the launch of the displayed forecast in September 2026. In both, the attribution factual and the counterfactual are free simulations from the same state.

The intervals of the attributable number and fraction, and of the exposed population of the ratio-of-means rule below, are posterior intervals of the ENSO effect. For posterior draw s, N F s and N C s are the mean window totals over the R/S replicates that ran s (Section S1.6.7), summed over the units of a scope at the same draw (the draw indexes the fit of the unit's ENSO group, so that a scope within one group sees one parameter vector), and AF s = 1 -N C s / N F s . These per-draw means still carry the epidemic variation of their replicates, so the variance of AF s across draws overstates the posterior variance by the within-draw noise, as in a nested simulation whose inner samples are the replicates of one draw [66], or in the analysis of variance that separates internal variability from the spread between climate-model ensemble members [67]. We write vnoise for the mean over draws of the delta-method variance of AF s , computed from the within-draw variances and covariance of the replicate totals, and vdraws for the variance of AF s across draws, so that vdrawsvnoise is the method-of-moments estimate of the posterior variance. Rather than report that variance alone, we shrink AF s towards the pooled value by max(0, 1 -vnoise /v draws ) before taking the 2.5% and 97.5% quantiles over draws; this rescaling keeps the centre and shape of the ensemble of draws and matches its variance to the estimate, the constrained Bayes estimator of a population of parameter values [68, 69], in place of the conditional-mean shrinkage that would narrow the ensemble by the full noise share. When the estimated noise share vnoise /v draws of a scope or first-level unit reaches 0.8, the replicates cannot separate its posterior spread from their epidemic variation. We then report its pooled value with the range of its replicates and no posterior interval; the figures draw that range dashed. The interval of the exposed population of the ratio-of-means rule covers the first-level units whose spread is identified, and the population of the others is reported separately, with the part whose pooled estimate passes the rule. Africa is computed like an ENSO group: the replicate totals of every simulation unit are summed within each replicate before the per-draw means, so that groups simulated together in one transmission unit keep their dependence. Draw s indexes the fit of each group and transmission units are simulated independently, so the sum at one draw index is a joint draw. Replicate quantiles of N F r -N C r (below) describe the spread of single paired simulations, epidemic noise included; so do the cumulative window totals of the main-text forecast figure, shown as the median and 50% and 95% quantiles across paired replicates, whereas its attributable fractions are the noise-adjusted posterior intervals above. Population counts use the 2025 population of the observed reporting units of each simulated first-level unit, and shares are of the population of the simulated first-level units in the scope.

Attributable burden.

The attributable number and fraction of cases or onsets are where N F and N C are means over replicates and RR is the ratio of expectations of eq. ( S27), so that AF coincides with the FAR of that equation.

These are the attributable number and fraction of health impact attribution [70, 71, 72]. We also report the replicate quantiles of N F r -N C r and of (N F r -N C r )/N F r , and the attributable number per 100,000 population.

Probability ratio.

For an outbreak event E we compare its probability in the two arms, the probability ratio of event attribution [73] and the probabilities that the ENSO forecast was a necessary, a sufficient, and a necessary and sufficient cause of the event [74]; PN is the fraction of attributable risk of event attribution.

We keep the sign of PN, PS and PNS, so that negative values indicate an event made less likely. We use two events: at least one outbreak onset in the window, and window cases of at least 50, the outbreak-size threshold of the episode database. When no counterfactual replicate has the event while some factual replicates do, PR is infinite; we then report as lower bound p 1 /p u 0 , where p u0 = 1 -0.05 1/R is the exact one-sided 95% upper bound on p 0 given zero events in R replicates [75, 76]. PS is undefined when p0 = 1. In large scopes both arms almost always contain the event and PR tends to one, so we interpret the probability ratio at the first-level-unit and country level.

Population with a larger expected burden. Following Childs et al. [72], a first-level unit counts towards the exposed population at magnitude m ∈ {0.25, 0.5, 1} when its mean factual total is positive and at least (1 + m) times its mean counterfactual total; a positive factual mean against a zero counterfactual mean counts, and two zero means do not. We report the posterior mean and 95% credible interval of this population, applying the rule to each draw's shrunk AF s , using N F ≥ (1 + m) N C ⇔ AF ≥ m/(1 + m). We also report the rule on replicate medians, which is not driven by the few replicates with very large outbreaks; it has no interval.

Population with a robust change.

Following the robustness assessment of the IPCC AR6 Atlas [77], with m as the variability threshold, a unit has a robust increase when at least 66% of the paired replicates have N F j,r > (1 + m) N C j,r and at least 80% have N F j,r > N C j,r (ties counting as disagreement), a conflicting signal when only the first condition holds, and no robust signal otherwise.

Emergence of the signal.

With the paired difference ∆ r = N F r -N C r of (S28), the signal-to-noise ratio where s ∆ is the standard deviation of ∆ r across replicates, measures the change relative to the epidemic variability that remains after pairing [78].

We report the population of the units in which the signal has emerged, S/N ≥ γ for an increase, S/N ≤ -γ for a decrease, at γ = 1 and γ = 2. Unlike the robustness classes, this measure needs no magnitude threshold; for a normally distributed difference, S/N = 1 corresponds to an increase in 84% of the replicates.

The first two metrics quantify how much burden, and how much more likely an outbreak, the forecast ENSO state implies; the last three locate the population where the expected burden is higher by a margin, where the replicates agree on the change, and where the change stands out from the epidemic noise.

S1.6.15 Testing of predictions for 2026 cholera incidence (West)

For the West group we compare the modelled ENSO effect on 2026 with the WHO national counts (fig. S26). The modelled ratio is the mean of the historical reconstruction over the mean of its neutral-ENSO counterfactual, over the only months both arms cover, May to August 2026 (the counterfactual is launched from the reconstruction in May, and the reconstruction ends in August). Each counterfactual replicate is paired with the reconstruction replicate it was launched from, and the interval is the spread of the ratio across the reconstruction replicates. The observed ratio is the 2026 WHO count over the mean of the ENSO-neutral years since 2010, standardised to the 2026 population, with one pseudo-case on each side; a year is neutral when its mean ONI lies within ±0.5 °C. It has two estimators: the same calendar months (May to August), for which the WHO monthly series covers only the neutral years 2024 and 2025, and the 2026 year to date against full neutral years, a lower bound. Countries without a WHO series are left out rather than counted as zero, and Somalia, whose 2026 series stops in January, is censored. Over the countries with an observed ratio, the modelled West ratio is 7.0 (95% interval over replicates 3.9 to 14.4), against an observed 7.4 for the same months (Cameroon and Congo) and a lower bound of 7.0 for the year to date (Central African Republic, Cameroon, Congo and Chad; modelled 6.3 for these countries).

S1.6.16 Attribution of past ENSO events

We apply the paired design of the forecast attribution to three strong ENSO events of the study period: the El Niño events of 2015-16 and 2023-24 and the La Niña of 2020-23, attributed as one event (table S10). For each event we launch both arms in the month before the ONI crossed +0.5 (El Niño) or -0.5 (La Niña) and stayed beyond it until the event's first peak: September 2014, August 2020 and May 2023. For the 2020-23 La Niña, whose ONI returned above -0.5 in May to August 2021, this persistence applies to the first of its three winters only. Both arms start from the reconstruction state of the month before the launch (Section S1.6.8), which has seen the observations of the event, and are paired as in the forecast: same reconstructed state, posterior draw and random number stream, with the replicates and draws of the forward arms (Section S1.6.7).

The factual arm uses the observed ONI throughout, as the hindcasts do (Section S1.6.10), but runs over the whole event window from a single launch and is not scored. The counterfactual arm replaces the ONI from the launch month onwards with analogue trajectories from the pre-industrial control archive of Lenssen et al. [65], built as in Section S1.6.13 and joined to the observed ONI of the launch month. We draw from the neutral set, so that the counterfactual describes the seasons had the event not developed. The ONI of the months before the launch is the observed one in both arms, and, as in the forecasts, the ONI entering each model is clamped to its widened boundary knots (Section S1.6.11).

The 2020-23 La Niña is attributed as one event: one launch (August 2020, the month before the ONI crossed -0.5), one pair of factual and counterfactual runs per transmission unit, and counterfactual analogues neutral in each of the event's three La Niña winters (DJF 2020/21, 2021/22 and 2022/23). Later winters read by the reported months (DJF 2023/24) are not constrained. The event's counterfactual has 83 neutral analogues.

Results are reported for the whole event and for consecutive per-winter sub-windows.

Windows are set per country. The comparison window runs from the launch to the end of the country's cholera year containing the peak of the event (see Section S1.1.6), extended by the largest positive lag selected for its group across the three models, so that the lagged response of every model to the peak falls within the window (the hindcasts use the smallest lag instead, Section S1.6.10). For the La Niña, the k-th sub-window ends with the cholera year containing the k-th winter's ONI peak plus the same lag, and the next sub-window starts the month after; the sub-windows partition the event window, so their attributable numbers add up to the event's, but their attributable fractions do not. A transmission unit is simulated to the latest window end among its countries, and each country is censored at its own window end. As windows are set per event, the later La Niña sub-windows may overlap the window of the 2023-24 El Niño, and the burdens of the two events are then not additive.

We read the factual arm in two ways. The first is the free simulation with observed ENSO. The second replaces, over the whole window, the factual series of replicate r with the historical reconstruction of the filtered replicate it starts from (see Section S1.6.8). This anchors the factual arm to the observations, but it conditions that arm on data the counterfactual arm does not see, so that the contrast also contains the misfit of the model to the observations. The two arms of this reading also treat the index differently: the reconstruction does not clamp the ONI, whereas the counterfactual arm holds it within the widened knots.

As the reconstruction has 100 replicates, this reading has at most as many distinct factual series.

From each pair of arms we derive the attributable number and fraction (S29), the probability ratio and probabilities of causation (S30), the signal-to-noise ratio (S31) and the two population metrics, as in Section S1.6.14, with every replicate kept, the same scope totals and, for the free simulation, the same posterior intervals over draws (fig. S20); the reading with the reconstruction as factual is a point estimate. In the main-text figure, the bars of Africa and each ENSO group show the median of this noise-adjusted posterior with its interval, whereas the per-unit maps show the attributable fraction of the replicate means, a point value without interval. As both arms launch in the same month, there is a single comparison window per country.

To relate the simulated fraction to the observed burden, we also report the observed-anchored attributable number 29 where Y z is the observed total of scope z over its simulated unit-months, with its interval from that of AF z and Y z held fixed.

Observed onsets are taken from the occurrence dataset and observed cases from the monthly case-count dataset, using for each unit-month the largest total among its reporting levels (see Section S1.1.7). We report alongside the share of simulated unit-months in the occurrence dataset, as a measure of surveillance coverage, and the ratio of Y z to the mean factual total, as a check of the factual arm.

As a cross-check without epidemic dynamics, we compute the burden attributable to each event directly from the fitted models on the observed data [70]. For an observed unit-month (i, t) in the window with outcome y it (an onset in the occurrence model, cases in the monthly case-count model), posterior draw s and a counterfactual trajectory ONI drawn for that draw from the event's neutral analogue set, where b l and L are the basis columns and selected lag of the model (S3), ONI equals the observed ONI before the launch, and β (s) l,i combines the population coefficients with the country and first-level random slopes of unit i (the country-level coefficients for a unit without a random effect).

Both ONI series are clamped to the widened knots as in the simulations.
