We report the mean and 2.5% and 97.5% quantiles over 400 posterior draws of the fitted models (the simulations use 100 per model), which carry both parameter uncertainty and the spread of the counterfactual ENSO trajectories. This calculation uses every observed unit-month of a group's countries, including reports at coarser levels that the simulations do not represent, and it ignores the effect of ENSO on contagion and on the immunity carried between seasons.

S1.7 Software and reproducibility

We fitted all models in R [79] using Stan [49] through the brms interface [80] and the cmdstanr backend [81]. We constructed exposure bases with the dlnm package [82], computed approximate leave-one-out cross-validation with the loo package [53, 83], and ran the block particle filter with the spatPomp package [30].

S1.7.1 Use of AI-assisted tools

The analysis code (data processing, model fitting, simulation, postprocessing and figure code) was developed with the assistance of the AI coding agent Claude Code (Anthropic) between May and November 2026, using the large language models Claude Opus 4.6, Opus 4.8, Opus 5 and Opus 5.5, Claude Sonnet 4, Sonnet 4.6, Sonnet 5 and Sonnet 5.5, Claude Fable 5.1 and 5.2, and Claude Haiku 4.5 and 5.5 for auxiliary agent tasks. Working in the authors' code repository under their direction, the agent wrote and edited code and ran analysis scripts on the authors' workstations and computing clusters. The authors made all decisions on study design, data inclusion, model specification, priors and interpretation. Code changes were reviewed by the authors and checked with an automated test suite; code restructurings were required to reproduce prior outputs exactly. OpenAI Astra 6 was used for a secondary code review. AI tools did not generate, alter or impute any data: all reported estimates come from the statistical models and simulations described here. The analysis runs without AI tools from the publicly available code (Section S1.7.2).

S1.7.2 Data and code availability

The analysis code, the canonical outbreak episode tables, the derived model inputs, the monthly WHO national series and the GitHub data folder cited in this supplement are publicly available at https://github.com/GenevaIDD/enso_cholera_africa and archived at [Zenodo DOI upon acceptance]. Outbreaks were extracted from the Global Cholera Taxonomy Database with the OutbreakExtractR package, available at https://github.com/HopkinsIDD/OutbreakExtractR. The raw outbreak database is available from the authors on request, under its terms of use. The files of data S1 accompany this manuscript and are listed with their content under Data S1 after the references. The ENSO index, the ENSO forecasts, the pre-industrial ENSO trajectories, the population estimates and the administrative boundaries are publicly available from the sources cited in Materials and Methods.

S2 Supplementary Text

S2.1 Limitations

Although our analysis builds on the most comprehensive continent-wide database of cholera, it has several limitations. First, our data are suspected cases, with case definitions and reporting that vary between countries and over time. Where confirmation is reported, episodes with a confirmed case have onset-rate contrasts by ENSO phase broadly consistent with those of all episodes, where the data allow the comparison (table S4), which suggests that the suspected-case signal is not driven by non-cholera diarrhoea. Reporting that intensifies around outbreaks independently of ENSO would tend to dilute an ENSO effect rather than create one.

Second, the ENSO teleconnection groups and the lag of each model are selected on the data the grouped models are fitted to. The partition is the dominant mode of one of several nearly equivalent fits (Section S1.4.2), and the selected lags are often within two standard errors of others (Section S1.4.5). Because we select the lags on a fixed partition after the grouping pass, we do not propagate the grouping uncertainty to our ENSO effect estimates, although the overall patterns were consistent across the numbers of groups we tested, despite multimodality across MCMC chains. We also did not account for the Indian Ocean Dipole, which is known to act synergistically with ENSO on climate patterns in Africa; it could explain some of the remaining uncertainty in the ENSO effects and is a subject for further study.

Third, neither our inferential models nor our outbreak simulator account for external drivers such as conflict or displacement, nor for cholera prevention and control measures such as vaccination or water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) interventions. There is evidence that ENSO increases the risk of conflict, so we believe that our estimates represent a total effect of ENSO through these pathways; in the occurrence model, which conditions on recorded outbreak history and contagion, they are direct effects with respect to those terms. Reactive cholera control may have shortened outbreaks, reduced their size or prevented large outbreaks from igniting, possibly diluting the association between ENSO and cholera. Accounting for these interventions is challenging because unified continent-level data on their implementation over the study period are scarce, but including them in future analyses would improve our estimates of how ENSO is associated with cholera outbreaks in Africa. More technically, we use a single source of ENSO forecasts, one calibrated CFSv2 issuance, without multi-model or issuance-to-issuance uncertainty, continued past each group's firm horizon by analogues whose skill falls with the lead (Section S1.6.12). Larger ensembles would make the ENSO forecast more robust, but we believe this uncertainty is marginal: ONI values were already high at the time of writing, and there is little doubt that 2026-27 will be a very strong El Niño event, although the peak ONI value it will reach remains uncertain.

Finally, our hindcasts are in-sample: we initialise the simulator from a smoothing state that has seen the scored season, and we use the observed ONI rather than the forecast values available when the simulation is launched. We therefore consider the reported hindcast skill an upper bound, with real-time skill probably lower. We nevertheless know of no other systematic quantification of seasonal cholera predictions, and the fact that these simulations outperform climatology across several regions and lead times points to a promising avenue for ENSO-or weather-informed early warning systems for cholera.

Table S3. Outbreaks by ENSO state of the cholera year, Africa and WHO-AFRO subregion. Each canonical episode takes the ENSO state of its cholera year: the December-February Oceanic Niño Index of that year, El Niño at or above +0.5 and La Niña at or below -0.5, with no persistence requirement. Outbreaks: number and share of the block's episodes; outbreaks per year: episodes divided by the number of cholera years in that state over the record, the same for every block; cholera years (Africa): number of cholera years with an episode. Cases, duration (weeks) and attack rate (suspected cases per 1,000 population): median [interquartile range] over episodes; p: Kruskal-Wallis test across the three states, not adjusted for location, season or year, which the models account for; these summaries do not enter the model fits. Blocks: Africa and the World Health Organization African Region (WHO-AFRO) subregions; national-level (ADM0) records are excluded. Cholera years start at each location's seasonal trough (Section S1.1.6), so the January 2010 to November 2024 record spans 16 cholera years.

Supplementary Text

Figs. S1 to S26 Tables S1 to S11 References Data S1

El Niño-Southern Oscillation predictively shapes cholera in Africa

Center (CPC) [59], here the forecast issued in October 2026 with target months to July 2027, assigning one ensemble member to each replicate by sampling with replacement. Raw CFSv2 Niño 3.4 forecasts are biased, and in strong El Niño events they overshoot the observed amplitude. We therefore calibrate them with the probability-distribution and spread correction CPC applies operationally to CFSv2 Niño 3.4 [60], which we rebuild from the CFSv2 hindcasts because CPC publishes the corrected forecast only as a figure. The CFSv2 hindcasts that train the correction are taken from the North American Multi-Model Ensemble archive [61], averaged over the Niño 3.4 box: 360 start months from 1991 to 2020, leads of 0 to 9 months and 24 to 28 members per start; the real-time forecast is the 40-member CPC forecast above. As in CPC's procedure, the 1991-2020 sample pools hindcasts initialised before and after the 1999 change in the CFSv2 ocean initial conditions [62, 63]. For initialisation month o and lead ℓ f (written ℓ in the subscripts below), let F oℓ denote the empirical distribution function of all member anomalies of these forecasts (each relative to its own start-month and lead mean) and G oℓ that of the observed Niño 3.4 anomalies in the corresponding target months. A member anomaly x j of the real-time forecast is mapped to with values beyond the range of the reference sample held at its extremes.

The observations are the monthly ERSST Niño 3.4 anomalies whose three-month running mean is the ONI the statistical models were fitted on, so the corrected forecast is on the scale of the fitted ENSO exposure-response. The ensemble spread is then set to the error of the corrected ensemble mean in the same 1991-2020 sample, oℓ , where bars denote ensemble means, RMSE oℓ is the root mean squared error of the mapped ensemble mean against observations and σ 2 oℓ the mean raw ensemble variance.

We scale the raw member deviations rather than the mapped ones because, in an event outside the range of the reference sample, the range limit of (S26) maps all members to the same value and would remove the spread entirely; CPC avoids this with a larger real-time ensemble. Only the calibrated ensemble mean is therefore held within the range of the reference sample; the members spread around it without a limit. Mapped instead to OISST v2.1 observations [64], this procedure reproduces CPC's own corrected ensemble means for the January to June 2026 forecasts to within 0.05-0.13 °C at leads of 0 to 6 months, where CPC's correction itself moves the forecast by 0.2-0.7 °C. For the forecast issued on 8 October 2026, the ERSST-calibrated seasonal means are within 0.08 °C (root mean squared difference) of CPC's corrected forecast from October-December 2026 to April-June 2027, whereas the raw forecast exceeds it by up to 0.8 °C.

We join the calibrated members to the observed record. Months already observed take the monthly ERSST value; the CFSv2 analysis months that precede the first forecast month are used only where the monthly ERSST record has not yet been released, after shifting them so that the three-month mean of the latest published ONI season is reproduced. The ONI of each trajectory is computed as a three-month running mean, using the published ONI wherever available.

In the forward simulations, the ONI entering a model is clamped to an interval around its boundary knots. With lo and hi the lower and upper boundary knots of the model's ENSO basis (the observed ONI range of its group) and ϵ the clamp margin, the ONI is held within [lo -ϵ |lo|, hi + ϵ |hi|], with ϵ = 0.25 in the forecast, counterfactual, hindcast and past-event arms and in the analytic cross-check. Within this interval the natural spline extrapolates linearly beyond the knots; outside it the effect is held at its value at the interval bound. The calibrated forecast of the 2026-27 El Niño reaches or exceeds the upper knot of several fitted curves, where an unbounded extrapolation would be unsupported by the data. ENSO phases follow one rule set. The NOAA CPC episode rule (ONI at or beyond ±0.5 °C for at least five consecutive overlapping three-month seasons) is used for the ENSO-state shading of the figures and for the summary of the reconstruction by phase. The cholera-year state of an outbreak and the classification of analogue winters use a threshold phase at ±0.5 °C without a persistence requirement. The launch of a past event requires the ONI to stay beyond ±0.5 °C from the month after the launch to the event's first peak (Section S1.6.16).

Data S1. (separate file) Policy-relevant tabulated outputs, one machine-readable CSV file per table. Every other tabulated output cited in the text is in the GitHub data folder of the repository.

• T3a effects country occurrence.csv: ENSO effect on outbreak occurrence by country at each ENSO condition against neutral (posterior median, 95% CrI) • T3b effects country duration.csv: ENSO effect on outbreak duration by country at each ENSO condition against neutral (posterior median, 95% CrI) • T3c effects country monthly.csv: ENSO effect on monthly case counts by country at each ENSO condition against neutral (posterior median, 95% CrI) • T4a event af cases.csv: Attributable fraction of suspected cases per past ENSO event, Africa and by ENSO group (95% posterior interval; every replicate kept) • T4b event af onsets.csv: Attributable fraction of outbreak onsets per past ENSO event, Africa and by ENSO group (95% posterior interval; every replicate kept) • T5a forecast af windows.csv: Attributable fraction of the 2026-27 forecast, Africa and by ENSO group, by attribution window • T5b forecast af sets.csv: Attributable fraction of the 2026-27 forecast, Africa and by ENSO group, by counterfactual analogue set • D10 enso exposure concordant.csv: Expected share of the population exposed to an ENSO effect of at least m in two or more analyses, Africa and by ENSO group (mean, 95% CrI) • D10 enso exposure exceedance.csv: Expected share of the population exposed to an ENSO effect of at least m in any analysis, Africa and by ENSO group (mean, 95% CrI) • D12 enso exposure by country.csv: Population with a high-certainty adverse ENSO exposure (P (effect beyond 25%) ≥ 95% in at least one analysis), by country • D18 attrib exposure adm1.csv: ADM1 units with more ENSO-attributable cases or onsets in the 2026-27 forecast, with their population • D18 attrib exposure by country.csv: Population in ADM1 units with more ENSO-attributable cases or onsets in the 2026-27 forecast at m = 50%, by country • D18 attrib exposure scope.csv: Population in ADM1 units with more ENSO-attributable cases or onsets in the 2026-27 forecast at m = 50%, Africa and by ENSO group

Fig 2. Subnational ENSO effects on cholera outbreak occurrence, duration and incidence, and the population living in ENSO-sensitive areas. Both start from the same reconstructed state; each replicate runs the posterior draw of its starting reconstruction replicate (100 draws × 100 replicates per arm), and every replicate is kept. Fig S1.

Fig.S1. Analysis workflow. (a) Data preparation: sub-national surveillance records, complemented by WHO national data (1), give outbreaks (2) that are mapped to administrative units (3) and consolidated into one episode per unit and cholera year (4), forming the occurrence, duration and monthly case-count datasets (5); ENSO is measured by the Oceanic Niño Index at lags of -11 to +11 months (6). (b) Statistical models: a latent-class model partitions the countries into five ENSO groups (7); hierarchical Bayesian models are fitted for each group and analysis(8), at lags selected by cross-validation (9), giving exposure-response curves(10). (c) Simulation: a particle filter reconstructs the outbreak state of every unit up to August 2026(11); forecast and counterfactual ENSO inputs (12) drive forward simulations (13), whose difference gives the outbreak burden attributable to ENSO(14). Icons: Font Awesome Free 6.5.2 (Fonticons, Inc.), CC BY 4.0.

Fig.

Fig.S3. Data processing cascade. Records, outbreaks and episodes retained by each processing step, from the weekly records of the Global Cholera Taxonomy Database to the three analysis datasets (Sections S1.1.3 to S1.1.8). Side boxes give what leaves the cascade at a step: records outside the WHO African and Eastern Mediterranean regions; location-period episodes outside Africa; national-level location periods; and, before consolidation, national-level location-period episodes and those without a population estimate, later outbreaks of a location period in the same cholera year (left out of the occurrence dataset) and the episodes of the composite location periods. One mapped location period loses all its episodes at the step that removes national-level episodes and those without a population estimate, so the composite location periods are counted out of one fewer location period than are mapped. The cascade ends in canonical outbreak episodes and forks into the occurrence, duration and monthly case-count datasets, whose counts are those of the prepared datasets, before the duration and monthly models drop first-level episodes that overlap a second-level episode. Onsets that fall inside a running episode of an earlier cholera year of the same unit are not occurrence events, but their episodes remain in the duration and monthly datasets.

Fig S10. Non-ENSO terms of the monthly case-count model. Posterior ratio (exponentiated coefficient; point and 95% credible interval, log scale) by ENSO group and term. (b) Terms without one, shown for the ENSO groups only: seasonal harmonics and population elasticity. Circles are country-level values (population effect plus the country's random effect), diamonds the pooled population effect of each ENSO group; countries are listed under their group's pooled row.

Fig S12.

Fig.S12. Country-level ENSO exposure-response, occurrence. Incidence rate ratio of outbreak onset against the ONI at the selected lag of the country's ENSO group, relative to ONI = 0: posterior median (line) and 95% credible interval (band) of the population ENSO effect plus the country's ENSO random slope, coloured by ENSO group, log scale. Each panel is labelled with the country's largest class-membership probability pmax ( † below 0.6). Conditional on the other model terms; a direct effect given outbreak history and contagion.

Conditional on the other model terms.

Fig S15.

Fig.S15. Historical reconstruction against observations. (b) Onsets per at-risk unit-month by calendar month. (c) Distribution of episode durations (consecutive active months in a unit). (d) Fraction of surveillance-covered unit-months in an active episode, Africa and each ENSO group. (e) Onsets per at-risk unit-month among units not active themselves in the previous month, at three scopes (horizontal axis: the same first-level unit, another first-level unit of the same country, another country of the same transmission unit), when an outbreak was ongoing at that scope in the previous month (left) or none was (right). Reconstruction: median (point or line) with 50% and 95% intervals across replicates; observed: crosses. Panels a, b, c and e pool all modelled units in Africa. In-sample: the reconstruction is conditioned on the observations it is compared with (Section S1.6.9).

Fig.S26. Observed and modelled ENSO ratio of 2026, West group. Circles: countries; diamond: the West group (GW) over the countries shown. Dashed diagonal: equality. (a) Same calendar months, May-August 2026 against the neutral years 2024 and 2025 of the WHO monthly series. (b) 2026 to 20 September against full neutral years since 2010, a lower bound (arrows). Countries without a WHO series are not shown; Somalia, whose 2026 series stops in January, is censored. Cameroon's May-August neutral years are both zero, so its observed ratio in panel a reflects the pseudo-count and has no interval. The reconstruction is filtered on the same WHO series (Section S1.6.8).
